\title{\Large\bfseries\boldmath A Note on Residual Networks:\\ Skip Connections Unlock $\dm^2$ Parameters per Layer}

\vspace{-1.5em}

% ===== sections/abstract.tex =====
\begin{abstract}
\noindent We investigate the geometric and representational consequences of skip connections. We find that at equal and even similar hidden widths, the function space they generate is generically different from the skipless one. Furthermore, we prove that in networks of attention and $\tanh$, GELU, SwiGLU or GeGLU blocks, skip connections increase the functional dimension over the skipless case by at least $\dm^2$ per block at any depth without normalization (resp. $\dm(\dm-1)/2$ under pre-RMSNorm). Without normalization, the increase is exactly $\dm^2$ per block in networks of $\tanh$ blocks or of GELU blocks of width at least $2\dm$. We show how to explicitly remove the $\#Layers \times O(\dm^2)$ parameters in the skipless case. Finally, by explicitly removing $O(\dm^2)$ parameters in residual networks, a counterexample to the statement of \citet{wang2025gauge} that the symmetry group of a transformer is the product of the groups of its blocks is provided.

% \noindent 1. The introduction and subsequent theoretical treatment of skip connections in the conventional, width-rich regime, has been largely centered around the benefits it brings to the optimization process of deep neural networks. We investigate the geometric and representational differences and similarities induced by the residuals with respect to their skipless counterpart. Our contributions can be categorized into three families: 1. Functional dimension differences; 2. exact and approximate equality of the families of MLPs with and without the skip connection; as well as 3. principled guidelines for parameter efficiency of both trained-residual and skipless networks. First we observe that skip connections inadvertently increase the functional dimension of MLPs by $\mathcal{O}(d_{model}^2)$ per layer. We then show that skipless and residual MLPs are generically disjoint classes of functions at equal width, and surprisingly the generic disjointness persists through a non-negligible perturbation of the hidden layer width. Finally explicit ways of collapsing the gauges (and thereby explicitly decreasing the number of parameters) in skipless and residual networks with learned linear residuals are presented. The observations are derived by formalizing residual MLPs with a learned linear residual through the lens of standard MLPs with hybrid activations, and explicitly collapsing the continuous $GL(d_{model})$ (resp. $O(d_{model})$) symmetries thereby also providing an effortless extension to attention layers. As a consequence, we provide a global gauge collapse reducing the total number of parameters in trained deep residual networks by $\mathcal{O}(d_{model}^2)$, which serves as a counterexample to the statement of \citet{wang2025gauge} that the symmetry group of a transformer is the product of the groups of its blocks, and provide a free-lunch parameter reduction by $8.5$\% of the skipless ViT-B of \citet{ji2026cutting}.

% \noindent 2. The introduction and subsequent theoretical treatment of skip connections has been largely centered around the benefits it brings to the optimization process of deep neural networks; we ask what they change in parameter space. Give each block of a network $\dm$ identity units, read and written by trainable matrices. Nothing nonlinear happens between that read and that write, nor, without skips, between the write of one block and the read of the next: both are linear junctions, where a change of basis preserves the function. Without normalization, spending these changes of basis turns generic identity units, and any invertible learned skips, into identity skips, so a skip connection is a gauge fix. The fix also ties the hidden states together: a residual network keeps a single change of basis for all its hidden states, while the skipless network with the same parameters keeps one per hidden state. Against it, the skips of $L$ blocks remove $L\dm^2$ dimensions of symmetry, and $L\dm(\dm-1)/2$ under RMSNorm, where learned skips reduce to triangular or symmetric matrices and the identity skip is both a gauge fix and a constraint. Where the symmetries are the only redundancy, raw parameter counts thus understate the effective size of residual networks against skipless ones. Every change of basis a network keeps can be spent after training to remove parameters exactly: $\dm(\dm-1)/2$ parameters per hidden state of a skipless network, $8.5$ percent of the skipless ViT-B of \citet{ji2026cutting} assuming standard pre-LayerNorm blocks. The change of basis that the skips leave is a global symmetry, which gives counterexamples to the statement of \citet{wang2025gauge} that the symmetry group of a transformer is the product of the groups of its blocks; with an untied readout it also removes, for example, $0.03$ to $0.10$ percent of the parameters of Llama-3.1. For one block, attention included, we prove with elementary tools that the removed symmetry is a gain in functional dimension, and we measure it in deeper MLPs and small transformers.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

% ===== sections/intro.tex =====
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

Skip connections are a building block of almost every deep network trained today \citep{he2016resnet}. The rationale behind the choice in deployed models is stated to be optimization and signal propagation: they make deep networks easier to train \citep{he2016resnet,balduzzi2017shattered,orhan2018skip,macdonald2023skip}.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/spectra_gelu.pdf}
\caption{Singular values of the Jacobian of the parameter-to-output map, with and without skips at the same weights ($L = 3$ GELU blocks, $\dm = 4$; median and range over $20$ draws of $\mathcal N(0, 1/\text{fan-in})$ weights and $\mathcal N(0, 1)$ inputs). The skips add $L\dm^2$ to its rank, the functional dimension, and $L\dm(\dm-1)/2$ under RMSNorm. Details in \cref{fig:spectra}.}
\label{fig:gelu}
\end{figure}

\label{fig:decision}
\end{figure}

Recent skipless models challenge their practical necessity. \citet{ji2026cutting} remove every skip from a ViT-B, a vision transformer \citep{dosovitskiy2021an}, and train it from scratch on ImageNet-1k \citep{russakovsky2015imagenet}, outperforming the original model in terms of top-1 accuracy. In language modeling, \citet{he2023shortcuts, he2024simplifying} manage to train transformer variants without skips and/or normalization.

On the representational side, \citet{lin2018resnet} show that deep single-neuron residual networks with biases are $L^1$ universal approximators. The result is however limited to the narrow regime with respect to the hidden layers and requires biases, which are excluded in a large portion of both academic and deployed models \citep{olmo2025olmo2,yang2025qwen3,liu2026ministral3,mistral2026small4,karpathy2023nanogpt} (see \cref{app:universal} for more details).

In this work we investigate what differences the skip connections induce in wide-hidden-layer MLP and attention layers. Do residual networks compute functions that skipless networks of the same widths cannot? And how many more directions in function space do their parameters reach?

Fix $\dm \ge 2$. Let $\mathcal S$ (skipless) and $\mathcal R$ (residual) be the sets of functions $x \mapsto h_LD$ computed by networks of $L$ MLP or attention blocks of fixed kinds and widths, without and with skip connections, and without biases or normalization: $h_0 = xE$, then $h_\ell = B_\ell(h_{\ell-1})$ in $\mathcal S$ and $h_\ell = h_{\ell-1} + B_\ell(h_{\ell-1})$ in $\mathcal R$, over all embeddings $E$, blocks $B_\ell$ and readouts $D$ (\cref{sec:setup}). For a single MLP block $B$ with $N \ge \dm$ hidden units, write $\mathrm{Id} + B$ for the residual block $h \mapsto h + B(h)$, and let $\mathcal S_{N'}$ be the set of maps computed by blocks of the same kind with $N'$ hidden units and no skip. Then
\[
\begin{array}{@{}l@{\enspace}l@{\quad}l@{}}
\text{networks, any blocks:} & \mathcal S \subseteq \overline{\mathcal R} & \text{Prop.~\rpref{prop:limits}{a}}\\[3pt]
\text{smooth blocks, any depth:} & \mathcal S \cap \mathcal R \text{ has measure zero in } \mathcal R & \text{Thm.~\rpref{thm:depthgelu}{b}}\\[3pt]
\text{ReLU, first block under $2\dm$ units:} & \mathcal S \cap \mathcal R \text{ has measure zero in } \mathcal R & \text{Thm.~\ref{thm:relufirst}}\\[3pt]
\text{ReLU, GELU, almost every $B$:} & \mathrm{Id} + B \in \mathcal S_{N+\dm} \setminus \mathcal S_{N+\dm-1} & \text{Thm.~\rpref{thm:relugelu}{b}}\\[3pt]
\text{ReLU$^2$, ReGLU, SwiGLU, GeGLU, every $B$:} & \mathrm{Id} + B \notin \mathcal S_{N'} \text{ for every } N' & \text{Thm.~\rpref{thm:homgated}{a}}
\end{array}
\]
Here smooth blocks are attention and $\tanh$, GELU, SwiGLU or GeGLU blocks, and $\overline{\mathcal R}$ is the closure of $\mathcal R$ under uniform convergence on bounded sets of inputs. $\mathcal S \cap \mathcal R$ has measure zero in $\mathcal R$ when the residual networks that compute a function of $\mathcal S$ have their weights in a set of Lebesgue measure zero, and almost every $B$ means every $B$ outside such a set.

The first relation holds because scaling each write up by a power of $t$, and the next read down by as much, leaves a skipless network unchanged and makes the skips of a residual network negligible, with an error of order $1/t$ (\cpref{prop:limits}{a}, \cref{rem:limitrate}). The second holds for $d_{\mathrm{in}}, d_{\mathrm{out}} \ge \dm$: a skipless network keeps a change of basis at every hidden state, so its functional dimension stays below that of the residual networks (\cpref{thm:depthgelu}{b}). The third holds because near almost every input a generic residual ReLU network is linear of rank $\dm$, while a skipless one uses disjoint units of its first block at an input and at its negative (\cref{thm:relufirst}). The last two say that a skip is worth exactly $\dm$ hidden ReLU or GELU units, and more than any number of the other units (\cpref{thm:relugelu}{b}, \cpref{thm:homgated}{a}).

\Cref{sec:setup} provides a common framework for the standard MLP, the MLP with a skip connection (including one with learned weights) as well as the attention layer.

In \cref{sec:gauge} we realize skip connections as a gauge collapse of larger networks: networks whose blocks have $\dm$ more units with the identity as activation, read with a matrix $U$ and written with a matrix $V$ (\cref{fig:junctions}a). Only $UV$ enters the function, so $(U, V) \mapsto (UK, K^{-1}V)$ changes nothing for every invertible $K$. The same holds at each hidden state of a skipless network, between the write of one block and the read of the next (\cref{fig:junctions}b). Without normalization, spending these changes of basis from the readout back sets every $U$ and $V$ to the identity whenever every $UV$ is invertible. The result is the residual network, with the same function and the same functional dimension, the number of independent directions in which the parameters move the function \citep{grigsby2022functional} (\cref{thm:gauge}). A skip connection is thus a gauge fix, in the language in which \citet{hashimoto2024unification} describe parameter redundancies. The fix ties the hidden states together: the residual network keeps one change of basis for all of them (\cref{fig:junctions}c), and the skipless network with the same parameters keeps one per hidden state. With $L$ blocks the skips therefore remove $L\dm^2$ dimensions of symmetry, and $L\dm(\dm-1)/2$ under RMSNorm (\cref{cor:count}). The skipless ViT-B of \citet{ji2026cutting} thus does more with less: it outperforms the residual ViT-B with about $8$ percent less functional dimension.

In \cref{sec:norm} we extend the collapse to normalized networks. Only orthogonal changes of basis pass through RMSNorm, so invertible learned skips reduce to triangular or symmetric positive definite matrices, and the identity skip is both a gauge fix and a constraint (\cref{thm:rms}).

In \cref{sec:reduce} we spend the changes of basis that a trained network keeps to remove parameters without changing its function. Without skips, the change of basis at each hidden state sets one $\dm\times\dm$ block of a read or of a write to the identity, or to a triangular matrix under normalization (\cpref{prop:normalform}{a}). For the skipless ViT-B of \citet{ji2026cutting}, assuming standard pre-LayerNorm blocks, this removes $8.5$ percent of its parameters. With skips, one global change of basis is left. When the embedding and the readout count among the parameters, it is a counterexample to the statement of \citet{wang2025gauge} that the symmetry group of a transformer is the product of the groups of its blocks (\cref{sec:global}). With an untied readout it also removes $0.10$, $0.05$ and $0.03$ percent of the parameters of Llama-3.1-8B, -70B and -405B \citep{grattafiori2024llama3}.

In \cref{sec:gain} we ask when the removed symmetry is a gain in functional dimension. It is when the symmetries are the only redundancy of both networks, the equality condition. We prove this for networks with one block, attention included, with linear algebra and limits along rays (\cref{thm:onelayer}). For networks of smooth blocks we prove it at any depth for the residual network, so the skips add at least $L\dm^2$, and at least $L\dm(\dm-1)/2$ under RMSNorm (\cpref{cor:resdepth}{b}). Without normalization, in networks of $\tanh$ blocks or of GELU blocks of width at least $2\dm$, they add exactly $L\dm^2$ (\cref{cor:exactgain}). \Cref{fig:gelu} shows the gain in the singular values of the Jacobian.

\Cref{tab:overview} and \cref{fig:decision} summarize what is proved for each kind of block, and \cref{tab:results} lists the results with their support. \Cref{sec:related} discusses related work, and \cref{sec:outlook} separates what is proved, measured and conjectured.
